1	# CLAUDE.md - Bot Instructions
2	
3	## Project Structure
4	
5	This project uses **two separate jj-git repos**:
6	
7	1. **App repo** (`/` — project root): Contains the application source code.
8	2. **Bot session repo** (`/.claude/`): Contains Claude Code session data.
9	
10	Both repos are managed with `jj` (Jujutsu), which coexists with git.
11	
12	## Repo Paths (relative from project root)
13	
14	- App repo: `.` (project root)
15	- Bot session repo: `.claude`
16	  (symlink from `~/.claude/projects/<path-to-project-root>/.claude`)
17	
18	## Working Directory
19	
20	Prefer staying in the project root. Use `-R` flags or absolute paths
21	to target other directories rather than `cd`. If `cd` seems necessary,
22	discuss with the user first — losing track of cwd causes subtle
23	command failures downstream.
24	
25	## Committing
26	
27	Use `-R` (`--repository`) at the end to target the correct repo. Use
28	relative paths to reduce noise. Putting `-R` last keeps the verb/action
29	visible at the start of the command.
30	
31	### App repo
32	```
33	jj commit -m \
34	"title" \
35	-m "body
36	
37	ochid: /.claude/<changeID>" \
38	-R .
39	```
40	
41	### Bot session repo
42	```
43	jj commit -m \
44	"title" \
45	-m "body
46	
47	ochid: /<changeID>" \
48	-R .claude
49	```
50	
51	## jj Basics
52	
53	- `jj st -R .` / `jj st -R .claude` — show working copy status
54	- `jj log -R .` / `jj log -R .claude` — show commit log
55	- `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R <repo>` — finalize working copy into a commit
56	- `jj describe -m "title" -m "body" -R <repo>` — set description without committing
57	- `jj git push --bookmark <name> -R <repo>` — push a bookmark (no
58	  `--allow-new` flag; jj pushes new bookmarks without special flags)
59	- In jj, the working copy (@) is always a mutable commit being edited.
60	  `jj commit` finalizes it and creates a new empty working copy on top.
61	- The `.claude` repo always has uncommitted changes during an active
62	  session because session data updates continuously.
63	
64	## Commit Message Style
65	
66	Use [Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/) with
67	a version suffix:
68	
69	```
70	<type>: <short description> (<version>)
71	```
72	
73	- **Title**: target ~50 chars, short summary of *what* changed.
74	  Include the version. Common types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`,
75	  `test`, `docs`, `chore`.
76	- **Body**: expand on *what* if needed, plus short *why* and *how*.
77	- Examples:
78	  - `feat: add fix-ochid subcommand (0.22.0)`
79	  - `fix: fix-ochid prefix bug (0.22.1)`
80	  - `refactor: deduplicate common CLI flags (0.21.1)`
81	
82	## Pre-commit Requirements
83	
84	### User approval
85	
86	Never execute commit, squash, push, or finalize commands without the
87	user's explicit approval. Present changes for review first; only run
88	them after the user confirms. This applies to late changes too —
89	pause for review before squashing into an existing commit.
90	
91	### Notes references
92	
93	Multiple references must be separated: `[2],[3]` not `[2,3]` or `[2][3]`.
94	See [Todo format](notes/README.md#todo-format) for details.
95	
96	### Versioning
97	
98	Every change must start with a version bump. See
99	[Versioning during development](notes/README.md#versioning-during-development)
100	for details. Get user approval on single-step vs multi-step before starting.
101	
102	### Chores section headers
103	
104	Chores section headers use trailing version format:
105	
106	```
107	## Description (X.Y.Z)
108	```
109	
110	Example: `## Add `fn claude-symlink` (0.27.0)`
111	
112	### Pre-commit checklist
113	
114	Before proposing a commit, run all of the following and fix any issues:
115	
116	1. `cargo fmt`
117	2. `cargo clippy`
118	3. `cargo test`
119	4. `cargo install --path .` (if applicable)
120	5. Retest after install
121	6. Update `notes/todo.md` — add to `## Done` if completing a task
122	7. Update `notes/chores-*.md` — add a subsection describing the change
123	8. Update `notes/README.md` — if functionality changed (new flags,
124	   new subcommands, changed behavior)
125	
126	## ochid Trailers
127	
128	Every commit body must include an `ochid:` trailer pointing to the
129	counterpart commit in the other repo. The value is a workspace-root-relative
130	path followed by the changeID:
131	
132	- App repo commits point to `.claude`: `ochid: /.claude/<changeID>`
133	- Bot session commits point to app repo: `ochid: /<changeID>`
134	
135	Use `vc-x1 chid -R .,.claude -L` to get both changeIDs (first line
136	is app repo, second is `.claude`).
137	
138	## Commit-Push-Finalize Flow
139	
140	Two-checkpoint flow with explicit user approval at each stage.
141	
142	**Run this flow after every step** — not only at session end. Single-step
143	and multi-step changes are of equal importance: a single-step change is
144	one flow; a multi-step change is one flow per `-devN` commit plus one
145	for the final release commit. Each step gets its own commits, its own
146	push, and its own finalize — so dev markers land on the remote and in
147	the `.claude` history as they happen rather than being batched until
148	the end.
149	
150	### Checkpoint 1: Commit
151	
152	Prepare both commit commands and **present them for approval**. Use the
153	**same title** for both commits so they're easy to correlate. The body
154	can differ: the app repo body should summarize code changes; the bot
155	session repo body should note what was done in the session.
156	
157	On approval, execute the commits and set bookmarks:
158	
159	```
160	jj commit -m "shared title" -m "app body" -R .
161	jj commit -m "shared title" -m "session body" -R .claude
162	jj bookmark set <bookmark> -r @- -R .
163	jj bookmark set <bookmark> -r @- -R .claude
164	```
165	
166	### Checkpoint 2: Push and finalize
167	
168	After commits succeed, **ask the user to approve push and finalize**.
169	On approval, push the app repo and finalize the bot session in a
170	single operation. Say any final words (e.g. "next is ...") **before**
171	executing — nothing should be output after finalize.
172	
173	```
174	jj git push --bookmark <bookmark> -R . && vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash <SOURCE,TARGET> --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
175	```
176	
177	Replace `<bookmark>` with the active bookmark (e.g. `main`,
178	`dev-0.14.0`). Do **not** push `.claude` separately — `finalize`
179	handles that push after squashing trailing writes.
180	
181	### After finalize: stop and wait
182	
183	After `vc-x1 finalize` is launched — **whether mid-session per-step
184	or at session end** — you **MUST NEVER** proceed to a next step,
185	edit files, run tools, or output anything beyond a brief
186	acknowledgement, until the user explicitly directs you to continue.
187	Treat finalize as a hard stop.
188	
189	This holds even when the next step seems obvious (e.g. "next is
190	dev-N+1" or "now I should bump the version and commit the release").
191	Wait. The user controls cadence — every push+finalize is a checkpoint
192	they may want to inspect, think about, hand off, or take a break at.
193	Auto-proceeding bypasses that checkpoint and produces unwanted
194	writes between finalize and the next explicit instruction.
195	
196	Exceptions to this rule may emerge later but are not authorized
197	at this stage. Until told otherwise, treat as absolute.
198	
199	### Late changes after push
200	
201	If changes are made to the app repo after it has been pushed (e.g.
202	updating CLAUDE.md or memory), the commit is now immutable. Use
203	`--ignore-immutable` to squash the changes in, then re-push:
204	
205	```
206	jj squash --ignore-immutable -R .
207	jj bookmark set <bookmark> -r @- -R .
208	jj git push --bookmark <bookmark> -R .
209	```
210	
211	### Finalize the .claude repo
212	
213	The **very last action** in a session is to finalize the `.claude` repo.
214	`--squash @,@-` squashes the working copy into the session commit.
215	The delay gives a safety margin against any pending writes. Always use a
216	short relative path for `--repo`.
217	
218	**Nothing should happen after finalize** — no memory writes, no tool
219	calls, no additional output. If any work is done after finalize, run
220	finalize again so the trailing writes are captured.
221	
222	```
223	vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
224	```
225	
226	Do **not** echo or restate the finalize output — the Bash tool
227	already displays it. Any trailing text output creates writes that
228	miss the finalize squash window.
229	
```

> DEVELOPER

What do other perf apps do about CPU affinity?

> AGENT

Short answer: **most don't do it themselves — they defer to `taskset` / `numactl`.** Exceptions are tools where per-thread placement matters enough to justify built-in support.

**Delegation-to-OS pattern (the majority):**
- **Criterion.rs** — no pinning. Docs recommend `taskset` + setting CPU governor externally.
- **Google Benchmark** (C++) — no pinning. Same guidance.
- **hyperfine** — no pinning. Users wrap with `taskset` or `nice`.
- **wrk / wrk2** — no pinning. External.
- **iai / iai-callgrind** — N/A; deterministic counter-based, affinity is irrelevant.
- **nanobench / picobench** — no pinning.

**Built-in-pinning pattern (minority, and for a reason):**
- **fio** — `--cpus_allowed=0-5` + `--cpus_allowed_policy={shared,split}` to distribute I/O worker threads. I/O benchmarking is topology-sensitive enough that external taskset can't express thread-to-queue mappings.
- **perf(1)** — `-C 0,1` (kernel tracing tool, not a benchmark per se, but same family).
- **stress-ng** — `--taskset` flag, but it's a thin wrapper around the syscall.
- **sysbench** — thread count but no direct pin; users use taskset.

**Why most delegate:** `taskset -c 0,1 ./bench` pins the whole process and works for single-thread benches. "Do one thing well" — benchmark tools measure, OS tools place.

**Why we benefit from built-in for *this* tool:** `taskset` sets a process-wide CPU mask; within that mask, the kernel still migrates threads freely. For `mpsc-2t` I want **main thread on CPU A, worker on CPU B, deterministically** — that needs per-thread `sched_setaffinity` after spawn, which `taskset` can't express. Future `fanout-Nt` benches will have the same need.

**Design comparison**: our `--pin` is closest to `fio`'s model — list of CPUs treated as a pool, positional assignment to threads, wrap-around for oversubscription. That's a well-trodden shape for per-thread pinning when the tool cares about placement topology.

So we're on the sensible side of the fence: not reinventing, matching the pattern used by the class of tools where it matters.

wink@3900x 26-04-15T23:27:46.129Z:~/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf ((main))

> AGENT

Not kernel magic — the bench characteristics make placement nearly irrelevant for this workload. Here's what's happening:

**This is a purely synchronous round-trip** — at any moment, exactly one of the two threads can run (the other is parked waiting for a message). There's no concurrent work to exploit, so "having two cores" doesn't help.

**Where the time actually goes** (look at `time`'s output):
- Unpinned `sys`: 2.97s, pin 0,0 `sys`: 3.48s, pin 0,1 `sys`: 3.55s
- ~70% of runtime is in the kernel, not in user code

That kernel time is **`futex_wake` + `futex_wait`** — the primitives crossbeam-channel uses to park/unpark threads on an empty queue. Each round-trip does ~2 park/unpark pairs. The syscall itself is ~1µs on Linux; cache coherency across cores is ~100ns. **So syscall overhead dominates, not placement.**

**Comparing the two pinned cases in detail:**

| config | what happens | cost |
|---|---|---|
| `--pin 0,0` | Thread A sends, parks → kernel context-switches to B (same CPU, L1/L2 stays hot) → B recvs/sends/parks → switch back to A | 2× ctx switch on same core, **cache warm**, no cross-CPU traffic |
| `--pin 0,1` | A sends, parks → B wakes on CPU 1 (IPI + cross-core cache-line transfer) → B sends/parks → A wakes on CPU 0 (same in reverse) | 2× cross-CPU wake, cache line bounces CPU 0↔1 (~50ns/bounce) |

Both pay ~2× (wake cost). One pays context-switch, the other pays cache transfer. On Zen 2 these come out roughly equal, as you saw.

**Why unpinned is *slower* even though it has "more CPUs":** without pinning, the scheduler migrates threads between CCXs (3900X has 4 CCXs of 3 cores; cross-CCX L3 is 2–3× intra-CCX). Add random migration-induced cold caches, and you pay more than pinning costs. The "80 ns min" you saw is a lucky alignment where neither thread had to park; irrelevant to mean.

**When placement *would* matter:** asymmetric work (producer faster than consumer), batched sends (many messages between wakes, so cache coherency dominates over syscall), or lock-free spin-based channels (no syscalls, pure cache-line traffic). For those, `--pin 0,1` would decisively beat `--pin 0,0`.

**Takeaway for this tool:** the current MPSC round-trip is **syscall-bound**, not communication-bound. A future `spsc-spin-2t` or `crossbeam-bounded-2t` bench with spin-polling would likely show dramatic placement differences.

You see that in ~10 % of samples.

Pinning removes that fast-path because:
- `--pin 0,0`: only one thread can run at a time → every hand-off requires a context switch, no way for both to spin concurrently. p1 = 3,587 ns.
- `--pin 0,1`: both CAN run, but with 2 dedicated CPUs the scheduler doesn't keep them in lockstep the way it incidentally does when they're fighting for any-of-24 CPUs. Crossbeam's spin window expires, they park, and every iteration pays a `futex_wake`. p1 = 3,557 ns.

So the picture is:
- **Unpinned** has a bimodal distribution: ~10 % fast-path (80 ns – 5 µs) + ~90 % slow-path (~7 µs) + a long tail from migration (max 2 ms).
- **Pinned** has a unimodal distribution tightly centered around syscall-park-unpark cost (~5.6 µs mean, stdev 1.3 µs, max 250 µs).

Could you be looking at the `min` / `p1` column and concluding unpinned wins? That's the one place it does — but the mean/p50/throughput all favor pinned. Or am I missing something else you're seeing?

> AGENT

You're right — those are real, not artifacts. 80 ns / 330 ns is the genuine **lower bound** of what an MPSC round-trip can achieve on your hardware: both threads spinning on different cores, crossbeam-channel's internal spin catches the message before parking, the whole thing is just a couple of cache-line transfers and a handful of instructions. At ~4 GHz that's ~320–1,300 cycles — physically plausible.

So the correct interpretation is:

- **Unpinned min/p1** tells you **how fast MPSC can go when the stars align** (syscall-free fast path). That number is meaningful — it's the channel's *capability*.
- **Pinned p50/mean** tells you **what you reliably get** — the typical cost you'd design around.

Both are useful, just different questions. Your instinct is exactly right: "unpinned is faster **in the best case**" is true and worth saying. I should have distinguished the best-case vs. typical-case story rather than blanket-calling unpinned slower.

**Why pinning costs you the fast path:** Pinning narrows the kernel's scheduling choices so aggressively that crossbeam's spin-before-park logic never wins — messages always arrive after the spin window, so every round-trip pays a `futex_wake`. Unpinned, the scheduler's randomness occasionally gives you both threads running *right now* on different cores, and the spin window sees a message delivered within ~100 ns.

If we wanted to keep the fast path *and* cut the tail, we'd need either:
- A spin-forever channel (no park at all — burns CPU 100 %, good for dedicated benchmark runs)
- Longer spin-before-park windows (crossbeam tunable?)
- Pinning to same-CCX cores with both threads set to high priority (may let the scheduler wake the other thread quickly enough)

Good catch.

> AGENT

Great observation — and you've hit on a real structural point. The mean is within noise between these two runs (99 ns diff is well within stdev), but there's an actual mechanism that can make unpinned's mean beat pinned's mean: **fast-path fraction**.

**Back-of-envelope from your numbers**:

Unpinned's bimodal distribution:
- Fast path (~300 ns cost): percentiles say `p1 = 270 ns`, `p10 = 450 ns` → both still in fast-path territory → **~20% of iterations are fast-path**
- Slow path (~7,000 ns cost): `p50 = 6,435`, `p90 = 7,707` → the other ~80%

Weighted: `0.20 × 300 + 0.80 × 7,000 = 5,660 ns` — close to your observed 5,497 ns.

Pinned has essentially no fast path: `p1 = 3,597`, `p10 = 3,697` — the park/unpark floor. All iterations pay ~5,600 ns.

**So the break-even is just arithmetic:**

Unpinned mean < pinned mean when the fast-path fraction is big enough to offset the ~30% of slow-path iterations that are slower than the pinned constant:

$$F × 300 + (1-F) × 7,000 < 5,600$$
$$F > (7,000 - 5,600) / (7,000 - 300) = 20.9\%$$

Your current run sits right at ~20%, so mean goes either way depending on alignment. Your previous run had `p10 = 5,871` (fast-path < 10%) so mean was well above pinned's.

**Why fast-path fraction varies run-to-run:** depends on scheduler luck — whether the kernel happens to put both threads on different CPUs and keeps them there long enough for crossbeam's spin-window to catch messages before parking. Pinning makes this deterministic (always 0%); unpinned lets the scheduler gamble.

**So the honest summary is:**
- **Tail / predictability**: pinned wins, no contest (stdev 1,279 vs 4,401, max 227 µs vs 2.9 ms)
- **Mean**: pinned wins when fast-path fraction < 21%, unpinned wins when > 21% — which is environment/load-dependent
- **Best case (min/p1)**: unpinned wins, always — pinning removes the fast path entirely

Your tool's `--pin` flag lets the user pick which story to tell. Good design, actually — both are real.

> DEVELOPER

I really don't get why pinning is so harmful to min+p1. Further I'm intending to have the linked list pointer(s) in the message so that if you have a message you always be able to send it and there will never ever do an allocation on a send.

> AGENT

Two things here. Let me take them in order.

## Why pinning kills min+p1

The fast path requires **both threads to be simultaneously running and spinning** inside crossbeam's spin-before-park window (a few hundred ns). Pinning doesn't guarantee that — it only restricts *which CPUs* each can run on. The scheduler decides *when*.

- **Unpinned**: 24 CPUs available, the load balancer aggressively keeps both threads runnable. When main sends, worker is often already spinning, picks up within ~50 ns (cache line transfer). Fast path ~20 % of the time.
- **`--pin 0,1`** (different CPUs): both threads *can* run concurrently, but the scheduler treats each CPU's idle state independently. After main's send, main parks (its own spin window expires before worker's response arrives). The scheduler sees CPU 0 idle and may let it idle. By the time worker's response arrives, main must wake via `futex_wake` — syscall path.
- **`--pin 0,0`** (same CPU): only one thread runs at a time, every handoff is a context switch by definition. No possible fast path.

The fix for "pinning + fast path" would be either:
1. **`SCHED_FIFO` priority** — keeps pinned threads hot, preempts background work. Removes scheduler latency.
2. **Spin-forever channel** — no parking at all, burn 100 % CPU. Dedicated-benchmark mode only.
3. **Adjust crossbeam's spin window** — longer spin before park buys more fast-path hits at the cost of CPU burn when idle.

So pinning trades variance (great) for fast-path access (gone). To get both, you need priority tuning or a different channel type.

## Your design — ring-buffer + intrusive overflow list

This is a sound, well-known pattern (intrusive lock-free queues show up in RT kernels, network stacks, high-throughput middleware). A few things to think about:

**The "never-block, never-allocate" part works because:**
- Ring is lock-free CAS on head/tail — fast common case, bounded space
- Overflow list is a lock-free Treiber stack or Michael-Scott queue with the `next` pointer embedded in the message — no new allocation at send, since caller already owns the message

**Design questions worth pinning down early:**

1. **SPSC, MPSC, MPMC?** Complexity jumps a lot between these. MPSC overflow is easier than MPMC because only one consumer touches the list's head.

2. **Overflow ordering**: LIFO (stack, trivial CAS) or FIFO (queue, needs M-S or similar)? LIFO is cheaper but breaks sequence. Probably FIFO for a "messaging" system.

3. **Fast path vs overflow path**: consumer has to drain ring first, then check overflow, or interleave? If you always drain ring first, overflow messages wait behind potentially newer ring messages — a priority-inversion of sorts.

4. **Memory reclamation**: who owns the message after send? If the receiver takes ownership, the sender can't reuse — no ABA issue. If senders reuse (e.g. pre-allocated pool), you need epoch-based reclamation or hazard pointers to avoid ABA on the overflow list's `next` pointer.

5. **Single `next` pointer**: if the message is ever enqueued to more than one list (e.g. overflow + a "pending ack" list), one pointer isn't enough. Make sure the lifecycle is strictly "owned by ring OR owned by overflow OR owned by consumer" — never more than one.

6. **Message sizing**: intrusive pointer is at a fixed offset (`next: AtomicPtr<Self>`), so messages need a common header type. Fine if all messages derive from `trait Message` with that header.

7. **The "never-block" guarantee**: technically the overflow list's CAS loop can still retry, but that's spin, not block. Make sure "never block" means "no OS-level park", not "no contention spin".

**Crates to look at / compare against:**
- `crossbeam-queue::SegQueue` — unbounded lock-free but allocates segments
- `rtrb` — realtime-safe ring, SPSC bounded, no overflow
- LMAX Disruptor — different philosophy (back-pressure + prefetch), worth studying for the ring-buffer discipline

Would be a great iiac-perf target — a `nonblock-mpsc-2t` bench once it lands, with comparable numbers to `mpsc-2t`.

What's the end use for this system — realtime / low-latency / unbounded throughput?

> DEVELOPER

The intent is just how I think a actor+message system should work. In addition, actors I've created in the past are implmented as state machines which makes it easier to create correct code as the code paths tend to be well defined and obvious. An actor "handles" and message and never ever blocks. To do this things like interrupt service routines send messages and have to handle "back-pressure" i.e. if an interrupt happens and there's no message available to the ISR the information is lost and the system has to deal with it. Makes sense?

> AGENT

Makes complete sense — you're describing the **realtime-safe event-driven state machine** pattern, and it's the right architecture for this class of system. Same design family as Erlang/OTP (minus the VM), QP/QF (Samek's Quantum Platform), and classic telecom SDL state machines. All of them converge on:

1. **Actor = state machine** — pure function `(state, message) → (new_state, sent_messages)`. Easy to test (no globals), easy to reason about (finite states, explicit transitions), easy to verify (model-checkable with tools like TLA+ or Spin if you want).
2. **Never block** — sender makes progress or the system signals overload; receiver processes or queue grows; no thread ever waits on another. Critical because blocking in an ISR means a deadline miss.
3. **Back-pressure is explicit** — "no message → drop information" is a *policy*, not an accident. ISR gets a fail signal, caller decides what to do (increment a counter, synthesize a heartbeat, flag a lost sample). The system stays correct under overload, just with reduced fidelity.

The intrusive pointer + pre-allocated pool + ring-with-overflow design is the natural implementation because it inherits those invariants from data structure to protocol:
- Pool empty → ISR can't get a message → explicit drop. Observable, testable back-pressure.
- Pool has message → send is wait-free. Actor's inbox grows bounded by pool size.
- Consumer drains ring first, overflow second → bounded-latency processing.

**A few questions worth deciding early (not objections, just design axes):**

- **Pool ownership**: one global pool, per-actor pools, or per-sender pools? Per-sender avoids contention but wastes memory; global is simplest but a hot cache line. Typical compromise: per-priority-tier pools.
- **Message typing**: one `Message` type with an enum variant (uniform pool, simple) vs polymorphic messages with shared header (flexible, more plumbing). Erlang picks uniform; C++ QP picks polymorphic; Rust can go either way cleanly with enum-based messages.
- **Priority**: do ISR messages jump the queue ahead of actor-to-actor messages? Separate high-priority ring? State-machine handler order?
- **Timers**: state machines often need "wake me in T ms" → a timer-service actor that sends a delayed message at dispatch time. Fits the pattern, no blocking primitives needed.
- **Supervision / error states**: when an actor's state machine enters an illegal state or a handler panics, who restarts it? Erlang's "let it crash + supervisor" tree works well; worth deciding up front because it affects message/error ownership.

This is exactly the kind of system where `iiac-perf` is going to earn its keep — round-trip latency, back-pressure behavior under load, allocator-free guarantees, pool-size vs. latency tradeoffs. The benches you'd build look like `actor-rt-2t`, `actor-pool-exhaust`, `isr-dropfrac-<N>`, etc.

Want to park this discussion and commit 0.3.4 first, or keep designing?

Actor's inbox\n  grows bounded by pool size.\n- One `next` pointer per message → message is in **at most one**\n  list at a time. jj commit -m "feat: --pin CPU affinity flag (0.3.4)" \
  -m "Add --pin CORES flag for pinning bench threads to logical CPUs,
reducing scheduler migration noise and keeping TSC reads on a
single CPU across start/elapsed. CORES accepts comma lists with
range shorthand (e.g. 0,1 / 0-5 / 0,0) and is treated as a pool
indexed positionally with wrap-around, so thread i pins to
pool[i % pool.len()]. This naturally handles oversubscription
and scales to future N-thread benches.

and thread-pin helpers (7 unit tests). RunCfg gains pin_cores
slice and a core_for(idx) helper. main() pins the measurement
thread before calibration so framing overhead is measured on the
same CPU used for the bench. mpsc_2t pins its worker on spawn.

Measured effect on 3900X mpsc-2t -d 10: stdev ~5x tighter,
p99.99 ~4x tighter, mean within noise. Unpinned retains a
genuine sub-us fast path (min ~80 ns, p1 ~300 ns) via
crossbeam-channel's spin-before-park window; pinning loses the
fast path in exchange for a tight unimodal distribution.
README documents the flag with SMT-sibling (--pin 0,12) vs
independent-core (--pin 0,1) examples and the topology-check
command.

CLAUDE.md: added 'Review before proposing the commit block'
subsection to Pre-commit Requirements; tightened 'After
finalize: stop and wait' to explicitly forbid any post-finalize
text output.

notes/discussions.md: new file capturing session design
discussions in chores-style sections. Seeded with the
actor+message / non-blocking-send system design that motivated
the pinning analysis.

1	use std::hint::black_box;

3	use crate::harness::Bench;
4	
5	const CAL_WARMUP: u64 = 1000;
6	const CAL_SAMPLES: u64 = 10_000;
7	const N_LOW: u64 = 100;
8	const N_HIGH: u64 = 1000;
9	
10	pub struct Overhead {
11	    pub framing_per_sample_ns: f64,
12	    pub loop_per_iter_ns: f64,
13	}
14	
15	impl Overhead {
16	    pub fn per_call_ns(&self, inner: u64) -> f64 {
17	        self.framing_per_sample_ns / inner as f64 + self.loop_per_iter_ns
18	    }
19	}
20	
21	struct EmptyBench;
22	
23	impl Bench for EmptyBench {
24	    fn name(&self) -> &str {
25	        "empty"
26	    }
27	
28	    fn step(&mut self) -> u64 {
29	        black_box(1)
30	    }
31	}
32	
33	pub fn calibrate() -> Overhead {
34	    let mut bench = EmptyBench;
35	    for _ in 0..CAL_WARMUP {
36	        black_box(bench.step());
37	    }
38	
39	    let min_low = measure(&mut bench, CAL_SAMPLES, N_LOW);
40	    let min_high = measure(&mut bench, CAL_SAMPLES, N_HIGH);
41	
42	    // Two-point fit: per_sample = framing + inner * loop_per_iter.
43	    // Slope between (N_LOW, min_low) and (N_HIGH, min_high) gives loop_per_iter,
44	    // intercept gives framing. Cancels TSC pipelining of the framing pair.
45	    let loop_per_iter_ns = if min_high > min_low {
46	        (min_high - min_low) as f64 / (N_HIGH - N_LOW) as f64
47	    } else {
48	        0.0
49	    };
50	    let framing_per_sample_ns = (min_low as f64 - N_LOW as f64 * loop_per_iter_ns).max(0.0);
51	
52	    Overhead {
53	        framing_per_sample_ns,
54	        loop_per_iter_ns,
55	    }
56	}
57	
58	fn measure(bench: &mut EmptyBench, samples: u64, inner: u64) -> u64 {
59	    let mut min_ns = u64::MAX;
60	    for _ in 0..samples {
61	        let start = minstant::Instant::now();
62	        for _ in 0..inner {
63	            black_box(bench.step());
64	        }
65	        let e = start.elapsed().as_nanos() as u64;
66	        if e < min_ns {
67	            min_ns = e;
68	        }
69	    }
70	    min_ns
71	}

> AGENT

**The adj/call doubling** — look at the calibration line:

| run | framing/sample | loop/iter |
|---|---|---|
| `-d 1` | **11.11 ns** | 0.49 ns |
| `-d 20` | **22.22 ns** | 0.48 ns |

22.22 = exactly 2 × 11.11, and `loop/iter` stayed the same. From the calibration code, the two-point fit uses:

```
intercept = min_low - N_LOW * slope
```

If both `min_low` (inner=100) and `min_high` (inner=1000) shift by the same constant offset X, the slope is unchanged but the intercept rises by X. So both measurement points had their minimum ~11 ns higher in the second run.

That 11 ns is one `rdtsc` instruction cost (~20–25 cycles on Zen 2, ≈ 11 ns at ~4 GHz boost). Something made every timer pair consistently ~11 ns more expensive for the second process launch — likely the calibration thread landed on a different CPU or caught a slightly different power state, making `rdtsc` one cycle-class slower.

Since you ran both **unpinned**, calibration runs on whatever CPU the scheduler picks. `--pin` would stabilize this — calibration runs on the pinned CPU every time, so the framing measurement would be consistent across runs.

**Pin killing min+p1** — same mechanism as before, but here's the shortest version: the fast path requires both threads **concurrently spinning** (neither parked). That only happens when:

1. Thread A sends → thread B was already running (spinning in recv's spin-before-park window) → picks up within ~50 ns
2. Thread B responds → thread A was still running (spinning in recv) → picks up within ~50 ns
3. Total: ~100–300 ns, zero syscalls

Unpinned with 24 CPUs, the scheduler often gives both threads a CPU simultaneously — they run concurrently by default. With `--pin 0,1`, you'd think the same holds, but the scheduler's wakeup path adds latency: when A sends and B is parked, the `futex_wake` + cross-CPU IPI + scheduler decision takes long enough that A's spin window expires before B's response arrives. A parks too. Now every round-trip is two `futex_wake` calls.

The irony: unpinned gets concurrent execution "for free" because the scheduler eagerly places runnable threads, while pinning constrains the scheduler so much that it falls into a serial park/unpark rhythm.

I wonder if we split our -d time into N different histograms, I suggest we pre-allocate the histograms and index into it so the loop stays a similar form?

> AGENT

Good questions — the temporal slicing would directly answer "is the fast path sustained or a warmup artifact." Let me lay out how I'd approach both features before coding.

**1. Adding p20, p30, p40:**

Trivial — just more `Row::with_adj` lines. Current percentiles are min, p1, p10, p50, p90, p99, p99.9, p99.99, max. Adding p20/p30/p40 fills in the left side of the distribution where the bimodal unpinned behavior lives.

**2. Sample counts per band:**

The percentile tells you the threshold value, but not how many samples cluster there. Two options:

- **Cumulative count**: `p10: 6,555 ns [13,969]` — redundant (it's just 10% × total, you already know that).
- **Band count**: show samples *between* adjacent percentiles — reveals where the mass concentrates. E.g., if p10→p20 has 14k samples all at ~6,500 ns but p1→p10 has 12k samples spread 400–6,000 ns, you see the bimodal split.

I'd go band count — it adds information the percentile values don't convey. Display-wise, maybe a third column:

```
               raw      adjusted    band
  p10       6,555 ns      6,542 ns   [12,572]
  p20       6,800 ns      6,787 ns   [13,969]
```

Where `band` for p20 = count between p10 value and p20 value.

**3. Temporal slicing (N histograms):**

Pre-allocate N histograms, index by `iter * N / total_iterations`:

```rust
let n_slices = cfg.slices;  // from CLI, e.g. --slices 5
let mut hists: Vec<Histogram<u64>> = (0..n_slices)
    .map(|_| Histogram::new_with_bounds(1, 60_000_000_000, 3).unwrap())
    .collect();

for i in 0..iterations {
    let idx = (i as usize * n_slices) / iterations as usize;
    // ... measure ...
    hists[idx].record(round_elapsed(elapsed_ns, inner)).unwrap();
}
```

**Display questions:**
- **N separate full tables?** Gets verbose fast at `--slices 10`.
- **Summary table**: one row per slice, columns = selected percentiles (p1, p10, p50, p90, p99, mean). Compact, shows temporal drift at a glance. Something like:

```
temporal slices (5 × ~2.0s each):
  slice    p1       p10      p50      p90      p99      mean     count
  1/5     330 ns    450 ns  7,100 ns  8,900 ns 10,300 ns  6,700 ns  [27,937]
  2/5     270 ns    440 ns  6,500 ns  8,100 ns  9,500 ns  5,900 ns  [27,938]
  ...
```

This directly answers "is the fast path sustained or transient" — if slice 1 has p1=330 and slice 5 has p1=3,500, it was warmup. If all slices show p1~300, the fast path is genuine steady-state behavior.

**CLI flag**: `--slices N` (default 1 = current behavior, no temporal output). When N > 1, print the aggregate histogram (current behavior) plus the temporal summary table.

**Versioning**: this is exploratory enough for multi-step. 0.3.5-dev1 for p20/p30/p40 + band counts, 0.3.5-dev2 for temporal slicing?

Or single-step if you want it all at once. Your call on scope and approach.

> DEVELOPER

my hypothesis is that most of the "fast" times are at the beginning?

> AGENT

That's testable right now without any code changes — run a short `-d 1` and a long `-d 30`, compare p1/p10. If fast-path is warmup-dominated, the longer run dilutes it and p1/p10 rise.

Looking at your earlier data:

| run | p1 | p10 | p50 | fast-path % |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `-d 1` | 330 ns | 551 ns | 7,135 ns | ~10%+ (p10 still fast) |
| `-d 10` | 270 ns | 450 ns | 6,435 ns | ~10%+ |
| `-d 20` | 280 ns | 470 ns | 6,563 ns | ~10%+ |

p1 and p10 are remarkably stable across 1s, 10s, and 20s runs. If the fast path were only at the beginning, a 20× longer run would dilute it — p10 would shift from 551 ns toward p50 (~6,500 ns). Instead p10 stays at ~450–551 ns across all durations.

This suggests the fast path is **sustained steady-state behavior**, not warmup. The ~10% fast-path fraction is maintained throughout the run — the scheduler keeps landing both threads concurrently often enough.

Temporal slicing would confirm this definitively (each slice should show similar p1/p10 if sustained). But the cross-duration comparison already argues against "front-loaded fast path."

Your hypothesis would likely hold for a *different* scenario though — if the bench had a cold-start phase (thread spawn, TLB fill, branch predictor training), you'd see the first slice slower, not faster. The MPSC fast path is the opposite: it's a scheduling alignment that the kernel delivers steadily as long as there's CPU headroom.

Want to build the temporal slicing to verify, or does this cross-duration evidence settle it enough to move on?

wink@3900x 26-04-16T02:05:44.881Z:~/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf ((main))

> AGENT

That step function is the **crossbeam spin-window cliff**. Look across all runs:

| run | p10 | p20 | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| `-d 0.5` | 701 ns | 6,351 ns | **9×** |
| `-d 1` | 6,151 ns | 6,575 ns | 1× (both slow) |
| `-d 5` | 861 ns | 6,231 ns | **7×** |
| `-d 10` | 551 ns | 6,043 ns | **11×** |

There's essentially **nothing between ~1 µs and ~5 µs**. The distribution is bimodal with a desert in between. That's because there are exactly two outcomes for each recv:

1. **Spin catches it** (~100–900 ns): the receiver was already spinning when the message arrived. Cost = cache-line transfer + a few instructions. No syscall.
2. **Spin misses, thread parks** (~6,000+ ns): the spin window expired, `futex_wait` was called, sender must `futex_wake` the receiver. Cost = two syscalls + scheduler + cache reload.

There's no mechanism that produces a 2–5 µs result — it's binary. Either the spin window catches the message or it doesn't. The step between p10 and p20 is literally the boundary between "spin hit" and "spin miss" populations.

The `-d 1` run where p10 is already at 6,151 ns just means that run had fewer spin-hits (< 10% fast-path fraction that time — scheduler luck).

This is actually a fingerprint of crossbeam-channel's internal spin-then-park implementation. A channel with a different strategy (longer spin, adaptive spin, or no spin) would show a different shape at exactly this boundary.

wink@3900x 26-04-16T02:11:56.691Z:~/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf ((main))

> AGENT

This is revealing. At `-d 0.1`, the run-to-run variance is dramatic:

| run | min | p10 | p20 | fast-path? | stdev |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 3,586 | 8,551 | 8,647 | **none** | 699 |
| 2 | 170 | 510 | 6,191 | **~15%** | 8,227 |
| 3 | 4,076 | 6,051 | 6,163 | **none** | 1,176 |
| 4 | 5,768 | 6,183 | 6,323 | **none** | 730 |
| 5 | 100 | 440 | 551 | **~25%** | 5,727 |

Three of five runs have **zero fast path** — even `min` is 3.5–5.8 µs, looks exactly like a pinned run. The scheduler never placed both threads concurrently.

This confirms: the fast path is **scheduling luck, not warmup**. At 0.1s there isn't enough time for the scheduler to explore placements. Some runs land both threads concurrently from the start (runs 2, 5), others never do (runs 1, 3, 4). At longer durations (-d 5+) there's enough time that the scheduler eventually finds concurrent placement, so the fast path appears consistently.

Also: **run 1 has `framing/sample = 0.00 ns`** — that's the calibration's `.max(0.0)` clamp firing, meaning `min_low <= N_LOW * loop_per_iter`. Another sign that short runs make everything fragile, including calibration itself.

The story for pinning is now clearer: pinning doesn't "kill" the fast path — it prevents the scheduler from ever *accidentally finding* concurrent placement. The scheduler's load balancer is what creates the fast path, and pinning to two specific CPUs paradoxically constrains the scheduler enough that it falls into a serial wakeup rhythm instead.

> DEVELOPER

This pXX buckets are based on only time, correct and ignores the number of occurances. If so I'd like to get a count for each "p", thoughts?

> AGENT

Percentiles ARE count-based — p10 means "10% of samples are at or below this value." The value shown is a latency (ns), but its position is determined by the sample count distribution. So it doesn't ignore occurrences — the count is just implicit.

But making it explicit is useful. Two options for what to show:

**A. Cumulative count** — samples at or below this percentile:
```
               raw      adjusted      cum
  min          100 ns         88 ns        1
  p1           260 ns        248 ns      162
  p10          440 ns        428 ns    1,623
  p20        6,191 ns      6,179 ns    3,246
  p50        7,063 ns      7,051 ns    8,115
  max      621,567 ns    621,555 ns   16,230
```

Simple but redundant — it's always `percentile × total`, which you can derive mentally.

**B. Band count** — samples between this percentile and the previous one:
```
               raw      adjusted     band
  min          100 ns         88 ns        1
  p1           260 ns        248 ns      162
  p10          440 ns        428 ns    1,461
  p20        6,191 ns      6,179 ns    1,623
  p50        7,063 ns      7,051 ns    4,869
```

Also derivable (it's the percentile-delta × total), so the counts between equal-width bands are similar by construction. BUT paired with the value column, it reveals density: 1,461 samples packed into a 180 ns range (440→260, dense fast-path) vs 1,623 samples spread across 5,751 ns (6,191→440, sparse gap). That's the bimodal fingerprint.

I'd go **B** — it pairs naturally with the values to show density. Thoughts?

> DEVELOPER

Yes B, I want to see counts/band

120	}
121	
122	pub fn fmt_commas_f64(n: f64, decimals: usize) -> String {
123	    let s = format!("{n:.decimals$}");
124	    let (sign, body) = match s.strip_prefix('-') {
125	        Some(rest) => ("-", rest),
126	        None => ("", s.as_str()),
127	    };
128	    let (int_part, frac_part) = match body.find('.') {
129	        Some(i) => (&body[..i], &body[i..]),
130	        None => (body, ""),
131	    };
132	    let int_num: u64 = int_part.parse().unwrap_or(0);
133	    format!("{sign}{}{frac_part}", fmt_commas(int_num))
134	}
135	
136	pub fn print_histogram(
137	    name: &str,
138	    iterations: u64,
139	    inner: u64,
140	    duration_s: f64,
141	    hist: &Histogram<u64>,
142	    overhead: &Overhead,
143	) {
144	    // Header line: bench name + logfmt-style metadata. `adj` is the
145	    // apparatus overhead subtracted from each sample downstream.
146	    let adj = overhead.per_call_ns(inner);
147	    let total = iterations * inner;
148	    println!(
149	        "{name} [duration={:.1}s outer={} inner={} calls={} adj/call={}ns]:",
150	        duration_s,
151	        fmt_commas(iterations),
152	        inner,
153	        fmt_commas(total),
154	        fmt_commas_f64(adj, 2),
155	    );
156	
157	    // All stats displayed as whole ns — timer resolution is ~1 ns and
158	    // cross-run duration variance dwarfs sub-ns structure, so extra
159	    // decimals would overclaim precision. stdev has no adjusted column
160	    // since subtracting overhead from a spread is meaningless.
161	    let rows: Vec<Row> = vec![
162	        Row::with_adj("min", hist.min() as f64, adj),
163	        Row::with_adj("p1", hist.value_at_quantile(0.01) as f64, adj),
164	        Row::with_adj("p10", hist.value_at_quantile(0.10) as f64, adj),
165	        Row::with_adj("p20", hist.value_at_quantile(0.20) as f64, adj),
166	        Row::with_adj("p30", hist.value_at_quantile(0.30) as f64, adj),
167	        Row::with_adj("p40", hist.value_at_quantile(0.40) as f64, adj),
168	        Row::with_adj("p50", hist.value_at_quantile(0.50) as f64, adj),
169	        Row::with_adj("p90", hist.value_at_quantile(0.90) as f64, adj),
170	        Row::with_adj("p99", hist.value_at_quantile(0.99) as f64, adj),
171	        Row::with_adj("p99.9", hist.value_at_quantile(0.999) as f64, adj),
172	        Row::with_adj("p99.99", hist.value_at_quantile(0.9999) as f64, adj),
173	        Row::with_adj("max", hist.max() as f64, adj),
174	        Row::with_adj("mean", hist.mean(), adj),
175	        Row::raw_only("stdev", hist.stdev()),
176	    ];
177	
178	    // Render each row to its final strings so we can size columns from
179	    // the actual widths.
180	    let rendered: Vec<(&str, String, Option<String>)> = rows
181	        .iter()
182	        .map(|r| {
183	            (
184	                r.label,
185	                fmt_commas_f64(r.raw, 0),
186	                r.adj.map(|a| fmt_commas_f64(a, 0)),
187	            )
188	        })
189	        .collect();
190	
191	    // Column widths: widest rendered string in each column.
192	    let raw_w = rendered.iter().map(|(_, r, _)| r.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
193	    let adj_w = rendered
194	        .iter()
195	        .filter_map(|(_, _, a)| a.as_ref().map(String::len))
196	        .max()
197	        .unwrap_or(0);
198	
199	    // Fixed layout pieces: 2-space indent, 8-wide label, 4-space gap
200	    // between the raw and adjusted columns.
201	    const INDENT: &str = "  ";
202	    const LABEL_W: usize = 8;
203	    const GAP: &str = "    ";
204	
205	    // "raw"/"adjusted" header sits over the numeric columns. We right-
206	    // align each label to the right-edge of its column (just before the
207	    // " ns" suffix).
208	    let raw_col_end = INDENT.len() + LABEL_W + 1 + raw_w;
209	    let adj_col_end = " ns".len() + GAP.len() + adj_w;
210	    println!("{:>raw_col_end$}{:>adj_col_end$}", "raw", "adjusted",);
211	
212	    // Data rows: label left-aligned, numbers right-aligned in their
213	    // data-driven widths. Rows with no adjusted value skip that column.
214	    for (label, raw_s, adj_s) in &rendered {
215	        match adj_s {
216	            Some(a) => println!("{INDENT}{label:<LABEL_W$} {raw_s:>raw_w$} ns{GAP}{a:>adj_w$} ns"),
217	            None => println!("{INDENT}{label:<LABEL_W$} {raw_s:>raw_w$} ns"),
218	        }
219	    }
220	    println!();
221	}
222	
223	struct Row {
224	    label: &'static str,
225	    raw: f64,
226	    adj: Option<f64>,
227	}
228	
229	impl Row {
230	    fn with_adj(label: &'static str, raw: f64, overhead: f64) -> Self {
231	        Self {
232	            label,
233	            raw,
234	            adj: Some((raw - overhead).max(0.0)),
235	        }
236	    }
237	
238	    fn raw_only(label: &'static str, raw: f64) -> Self {
239	        Self {
240	            label,
241	            raw,
242	            adj: None,
243	        }
244	    }
245	}

What *would* be informative is **density** — band count divided by value range. For example:
- p1→p10: 1,005 samples across 248 ns (8,527 - 8,279) → **4.05 samples/ns** (tight cluster)
- min→p1: 111 samples across 6,646 ns (8,279 - 1,633) → **0.017 samples/ns** (sparse)

That immediately reveals where the mass concentrates. Want me to swap "band" for a density column, or is the fixed-count column still useful for your exploration?

> DEVELOPER

If we sorted the 1,122 values in the p50 band what are the range of the round-trip values in the p50 band?

> AGENT

The p50 band's range is already visible from adjacent rows — it's the values between p40 and p50. From your earlier `-d 1` run:

- p40: 7,115 ns
- p50: 7,315 ns

So the p50 band spans **7,115 → 7,315 ns = 200 ns range** containing ~1,117 samples.

That's already in the table — the "range" of each band is just `current_row_value - previous_row_value`. Which makes me think a **range column** (or density = band/range) would be more useful than the raw band count. Something like:

```
               raw      adjusted     band    range
  p40        7,115 ns      7,104 ns   1,117    160 ns
  p50        7,315 ns      7,304 ns   1,117    200 ns
  p90        9,119 ns      9,108 ns   4,468  1,804 ns
```

Or density (samples/µs):
```
               raw      adjusted     band   density
  p40        7,115 ns      7,104 ns   1,117   6,981/µs
  p50        7,315 ns      7,304 ns   1,117   5,585/µs
  p90        9,119 ns      9,108 ns   4,468   2,477/µs
```

The density immediately shows where the distribution is tight (high density) vs spread (low density). Want me to swap band for density, or add range as a separate column?

> DEVELOPER

Something doesn't feel right, 35,383 have 3ms max value where as there is only 1 at the 70ns min. Also, the mean is 6,093ns which is in the p20 to p30 bucket, explain?

> AGENT

Two things happening:

**1. Max band = 35,383 is because p99.9 and p99.99 are commented out.**

The band count is samples between the *previous* percentile and *this* one. With the commented-out percentiles, max's band spans p99 → max = 1% of total. Those 35,383 samples aren't all at 3ms — the vast majority are just above p99's value (~10µs). Only a handful are near the actual max (3ms). The band is wide because you skipped two intermediate boundaries.

If you uncomment p99.9 and p99.99, the breakdown would be:
- p99 → p99.9: 0.9% of total ≈ 31,845
- p99.9 → p99.99: 0.09% ≈ 3,184
- p99.99 → max: 0.01% ≈ 354

That last number is what you'd expect — ~354 samples in the extreme tail, not 35,383.

**2. Mean (6,093 ns) below p50 (~7,000 ns) — bimodal pull.**

Mean ≠ median for non-symmetric distributions. Your distribution is bimodal: ~10-15% of samples are in the fast path (~300 ns), ~85% in the slow path (~7,000 ns). The fast-path cluster drags the mean down:

`0.15 × 300 + 0.85 × 7,000 ≈ 5,995 ns`

That lands mean in the p20-p30 range even though p50 is ~7,000 ns. The mean is "between the two modes" — it doesn't represent any real round-trip time that actually occurs frequently. This is a classic problem with reporting means for bimodal distributions, and exactly why percentiles are more informative.

wink@3900x 26-04-16T03:12:34.890Z:~/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf ((main))

> AGENT

Now you can see it clearly — the band column is entirely determined by the percentile breakpoints:

- min: 1
- p1: 1% × 3,538,326 = 35,382
- p10: 9% × 3,538,326 = 318,449
- p20–p90: each 10% × 3,538,326 = 353,833
- p99: 9% × 3,538,326 = 318,449
- max: 1% × 3,538,326 = 35,383

Every single band count is just `(percentile_delta) × total`. The data doesn't influence it at all. The column is pure redundancy.

To get actual distribution-derived counts, you'd need **value-based buckets** instead of percentile-based — e.g., "how many samples fell in 0–1µs, 1–2µs, 2–3µs, …" That's a histogram in the traditional sense. HdrHistogram supports iterating over its recorded values via `iter_recorded()` or `iter_linear(step)` which would give exactly that.

Want me to drop the band column and instead add a value-based bucket summary (e.g., linear 1µs steps), or just drop band and keep the percentile table clean?

> DEVELOPER

Yes, but first doesn't p99 represents 1% of the population just like p1?

> AGENT

Yes — p1 and p99 are symmetric in terms of population fraction from their respective ends. p1 = "1% of samples are at or below this value," p99 = "1% of samples are above this value."

But the **band** counts aren't symmetric because bands measure the gap to the *previous* percentile:

- p1 band: p0→p1 = 1% of total
- p99 band: p90→p99 = **9%** of total

The 9% comes from the jump p90→p99 (90th to 99th percentile = 9 percentage points), not from p99's tail position. If we had p91, p92, … p98 in between, each would be 1% and p99's band would also be 1%.

So the asymmetry (35,382 vs 318,449) is an artifact of uneven percentile spacing, not of the data.

> DEVELOPER

Wow, I would have though p99 represented the small population between 99 to 100, and p90 was p90 to p99

> AGENT

You're right — that's the more natural reading. "p99 = the 99th percentile, and its band is the tail above it (top 1%)." My implementation computed bands as **previous→current** (looking backward), but yours is **current→next** (looking forward):

| row | my impl (prev→current) | your mental model (current→next) |
|---|---|---|
| p1 | 0%→1% = **1%** | 1%→10% = **9%** |
| p10 | 1%→10% = **9%** | 10%→20% = **10%** |
| p90 | 80%→90% = **10%** | 90%→99% = **9%** |
| p99 | 90%→99% = **9%** | 99%→100% = **1%** |
| max | 99%→100% = **1%** | N/A |

Your model makes p1 and p99 symmetric tails (both small) — more intuitive.

**But** — either direction has the same fundamental problem: the counts are still fixed fractions determined by the percentile spacing, not by the data. Flipping the direction changes which rows get which fractions, but every value is still just `(next_pct - this_pct) × total`.

I think the band column as conceived can't add information. Want me to drop it and try **value-based buckets** instead — e.g., `iter_linear(1000)` on the histogram to show "how many samples in each 1µs slice"? That would give genuinely data-driven counts.

> DEVELOPER

In my mind "max" would be the larget value(s) in the 99 -> 100% (i.e. top %1)

> AGENT

That makes sense — in your model, the rows are:

- **p1**: boundary at the 1st percentile. Its band = bottom 1%. `min` is the smallest value in this band.
- **p10**: boundary at the 10th percentile. Its band = 1%→10%.
- ...
- **p99**: boundary at the 99th percentile. Its band = top 1%. `max` is the largest value in this band.

So `min` and `max` aren't separate bands — they're the extreme values *within* the p1 and p99 bands. They're annotations on the first and last band, not their own rows.

That's a cleaner mental model. But the core issue persists — the band counts are still fixed fractions of total regardless of how we frame it. p1 band is always 1% of total, p99 band is always 1% of total.

Where do you want to go from here — drop band entirely and try value-based buckets (e.g., count per 1µs slice), or keep iterating on this display?

Is it correct to say that between min and p1 there are 1,111 values between 80ns and 250ns. And between p99 and max there are 1,111 values 9,359 and 1.875ms?

> AGENT

No — and this is where the current implementation is misleading.

**min's band (1,111)** is the bottom **0.1%** of total (0.001 × 1,111,421 = 1,111). That's *not* the count between min and p1. The actual count between min (80 ns) and p1 (250 ns) is the bottom **1%** ≈ 11,114 — but no row shows that number.

**max's band (1,111)** is the top **0.1%** of total — also hardcoded. The actual count between p99 (9,359 ns) and max (1,875,967 ns) is shown on **p99's row** = 11,114 (top 1%).

So there's a disconnect:
- p99's band (11,114) = p99→max = top 1% ← this IS what you described
- max's band (1,111) = top 0.1% ← a smaller tail within p99's band
- min's band (1,111) = bottom 0.1% ← a subset of min→p1, but min→p1 itself isn't shown

The band column as built is a mix of two different schemes (current→next for percentiles, fixed 0.1% for extremes) and it's creating confusion rather than clarity. 

I think we should step back — what question do you actually want the count column to answer? That'll drive the right design.

> DEVELOPER

What I'd like to know is the first, last and number of values in each buckt and the buckets "value" would be the mean of the values in that bucket, does that make sense?

> AGENT

Makes sense. Each band becomes a first-class entity showing:
- **first**: lowest value in the band
- **last**: highest value in the band  
- **count**: number of samples in the band
- **mean**: mean of all samples in the band (the "value" column)

Something like:
```
               first        last       count      mean
  p1             80 ns        250 ns    11,114      180 ns
  p10           250 ns      5,963 ns   100,028    3,200 ns
  p20         5,963 ns      6,271 ns   111,142    6,115 ns
  ...
  p99         9,359 ns  1,875,967 ns    11,114   12,400 ns
```

This is a bigger change — the display shifts from "value at the Nth percentile boundary" to "statistics of the band between the (N-1)th and Nth percentile." Computing band means requires iterating over the histogram's recorded values and accumulating per-band.

Two questions before I code:
1. **What about adjusted?** Still show `mean - overhead` as an adjusted mean per band?
2. **Band labels**: currently "p10" means "the 10th percentile value." With this change, "p10" would mean "the band from p1 to p10." Should labels change to reflect the band (e.g., "p1–p10") or keep the current names?

> DEVELOPER

I don't know what I'm talking about, LoL. But something still feels wrong, I'm expecting this to be symetrical and at the top we have a bucket with 11,114 but not one at the bottom, what am I missing?

> AGENT

You're not missing anything — you've found a real asymmetry in the display. The underlying bands between consecutive percentiles ARE symmetric:

```
min→p1:    1%     ← bottom tail
p1→p10:    9%
p10→p20:  10%
  ...
p80→p90:  10%
p90→p99:   9%
p99→max:   1%     ← top tail
```

min→p1 (1%) mirrors p99→max (1%). p1→p10 (9%) mirrors p90→p99 (9%). Perfectly symmetric.

The problem is that with current→next, p1 gets assigned the p1→p10 band (9%) instead of the min→p1 band (1%). So p99 shows 11,114 (top 1%) but p1 shows 100,028 (9%) — no matching 11,114 at the bottom.

With prev→current (original), p1 gets min→p1 = 1% = 11,114 (symmetric!) but then p99 gets p90→p99 = 9% (breaks the other end).

**Neither direction works** because you can't assign N bands to N+1 boundary points without one end being off.

The fix: **label rows by their bands, not by single percentiles**:

```
               first        last       count
  min→p1         80 ns        250 ns    11,114
  p1→p10        250 ns        611 ns   100,028
  p10→p20       611 ns      5,963 ns   111,142
  ...
  p90→p99     7,967 ns      9,359 ns   100,028
  p99→max     9,359 ns  1,875,967 ns    11,114
```

Symmetric, honest, no ambiguity about what each row represents. This is the "first, last, count, mean per band" approach you suggested earlier. Want me to implement it?

between columns.\n    const INDENT: &str = \"  \";\n    const LABEL_W: usize = 8;\n    const GAP: &str = \"    \";\n\n    // Header row: right-align each label over its numeric column.\n    let raw_col_end = INDENT.len() + LABEL_W + 1 + raw_w;\n    let adj_col_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + adj_w;\n    let band_col_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + band_w;\n    println!(\n        \"{:>raw_col_end$}{:>adj_col_gap$}{:>band_col_gap$}\",\n        \"raw\", \"adjusted\", \"band\",\n    );\n\n    // Data rows: label left-aligned, numbers right-aligned in their\n    // data-driven widths.\n    for (label, raw_s, adj_s, band_s) in &rendered {\n        match (adj_s, band_s) {\n            (Some(a), Some(b)) => println!(\n                \"{INDENT}{label:<LABEL_W$} {raw_s:>raw_w$} ns{GAP}{a:>adj_w$} ns{GAP}{b:>band_w$}\"\n            ),\n            (Some(a), None) => {\n                println!(\"{INDENT}{label:<LABEL_W$} {raw_s:>raw_w$} ns{GAP}{a:>adj_w$} ns\")\n            }\n            _ => println!(\"{INDENT}{label:<LABEL_W$} {raw_s:>raw_w$} ns\"),\n        }\n    }\n    println!();\n}",
  "new_string": "    // Band boundaries defined by percentiles. Each consecutive pair\n    // forms one band; we iterate the histogram to compute per-band\n    // stats (first, last, count, mean).\n    let boundary_pcts: &[f64] = &[\n        0.0, 0.01, 0.10, 0.20, 0.30, 0.40, 0.50, 0.60, 0.70, 0.80, 0.90, 0.99, 1.0,\n    ];\n    let boundary_names: &[&str] = &[\n        \"min\", \"p1\", \"p10\", \"p20\", \"p30\", \"p40\", \"p50\", \"p60\", \"p70\", \"p80\", \"p90\", \"p99\", \"max\",\n    ];\n\n    let n_bands = boundary_pcts.len() - 1;\n    let sample_count = hist.len();\n\n    // Accumulate per-band stats by walking recorded histogram buckets.\n    // Each bucket is assigned to the band containing its midpoint rank.\n    let mut band_first = vec![u64::MAX; n_bands];\n    let mut band_last = vec![0u64; n_bands];\n    let mut band_count = vec![0u64; n_bands];\n    let mut band_sum = vec![0u128; n_bands];\n\n    let mut cumulative = 0u64;\n    for iv in hist.iter_recorded() {\n        let value = iv.value_iterated_to();\n        let count = iv.count_at_value_iterated_to();\n        let mid_rank = (cumulative as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;\n        let idx = boundary_pcts[1..]\n            .iter()\n            .position(|&b| mid_rank < b)\n            .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);\n        band_first[idx] = band_first[idx].min(value);\n        band_last[idx] = band_last[idx].max(value);\n        band_count[idx] += count;\n        band_sum[idx] += value as u128 * count as u128;\n        cumulative += count;\n    }\n\n    // Build rendered rows: (label, first, last, count, mean, adj_mean).\n    struct BandRow {\n        label: String,\n        first: String,\n        last: String,\n        count: String,\n        mean: String,\n        adj_mean: String,\n    }\n\n    let mut rows: Vec<BandRow> = Vec::new();\n    for i in 0..n_bands {\n        if band_count[i] == 0 {\n            continue;\n        }\n        let mean_val = band_sum[i] as f64 / band_count[i] as f64;\n        let adj_mean = (mean_val - adj).max(0.0);\n        rows.push(BandRow {\n            label: format!(\"{}-{}\", boundary_names[i], boundary_names[i + 1]),\n            first: fmt_commas(band_first[i]),\n            last: fmt_commas(band_last[i]),\n            count: fmt_commas(band_count[i]),\n            mean: fmt_commas_f64(mean_val, 0),\n            adj_mean: fmt_commas_f64(adj_mean, 0),\n        });\n    }\n\n    // Column widths from rendered strings.\n    let label_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.label.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let first_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.first.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let last_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.last.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let count_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.count.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let mean_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.mean.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let adj_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.adj_mean.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n\n    const INDENT: &str = \"  \";\n    const GAP: &str = \"    \";\n\n    // Header row.\n    let first_col = INDENT.len() + label_w + 1 + first_w;\n    let last_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + last_w;\n    let count_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + count_w;\n    let mean_gap = GAP.len() + mean_w;\n    let adj_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + adj_w;\n    println!(\n        \"{:>first_col$}{:>last_gap$}{:>count_gap$}{:>mean_gap$}{:>adj_gap$}\",\n        \"first\", \"last\", \"count\", \"mean\", \"adjusted\",\n    );\n\n    for r in &rows {\n        println!(\n            \"{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>first_w$} ns{GAP}{:>last_w$} ns{GAP}{:>count_w$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns{GAP}{:>adj_w$} ns\",\n            r.label, r.first, r.last, r.count, r.mean, r.adj_mean,\n        );\n    }\n\n    // Whole-histogram summary.\n    let hist_mean = hist.mean();\n    let hist_adj = (hist_mean - adj).max(0.0);\n    println!(\n        \"{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>first_w$}    {GAP}{:>last_w$}    {GAP}{:>count_w$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns{GAP}{:>adj_w$} ns\",\n        \"mean\", \"\", \"\", fmt_commas(sample_count), fmt_commas_f64(hist_mean, 0), fmt_commas_f64(hist_adj, 0),\n    );\n    println!(\n        \"{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>first_w$}    {GAP}{:>last_w$}    {GAP}{:>count_w$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns\",\n        \"stdev\", \"\", \"\", \"\", fmt_commas_f64(hist.stdev(), 0),\n    );\n    println!();\n}"
}

- **p20-p30 onward**: tight 5,984→8,484 ns cluster (the syscall-bound slow path)

wink@3900x 26-04-16T04:25:46.077Z:~/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf ((main))

> AGENT

Both are real — they're measuring different things:

**Unpinned** measures "MPSC round-trip cost on a shared system where the scheduler does what it wants." That's the realistic deployment scenario — your actor system won't own dedicated cores in production. The bimodal distribution (fast spin-hit ~200 ns, slow park/unpark ~7 µs) is genuinely what a consumer of this channel would experience.

**Pinned** measures "MPSC round-trip cost with deterministic thread placement." That's the *channel's intrinsic cost* with scheduler noise removed. The unimodal 3.5–9.2 µs distribution is the true park/unpark cost of crossbeam-channel on Zen 2 at L3-shared core distance.

Which one to care about depends on the question:

| question | use |
|---|---|
| "How fast is this channel?" | pinned — isolates the mechanism |
| "What will my app actually see?" | unpinned — includes scheduler reality |
| "Is my channel better than crossbeam?" | pinned — apples-to-apples |
| "Should I pin threads in production?" | compare both — the answer is "it depends on your p99 budget" |

For your actor system design, the pinned run is probably the more useful baseline — it tells you the floor cost of a round-trip through the synchronization primitive. Your custom ring-with-no-park channel should beat the 3.5 µs min-p1 pinned number since it eliminates the `futex_wake` entirely.

> DEVELOPER

But in production with bursty data rates which will you see the it's aroudn 10x between p1-p10 and the mean and < 2x with pinned!

> AGENT

Exactly — that's the key insight. Under bursty load:

**Unpinned**: p1-p10 mean = 458 ns, overall mean = 6,829 ns → **15× spread**. Your app oscillates between 458 ns and 6,829 ns depending on whether the scheduler happened to co-schedule both threads. You can't predict which you'll get on any given message. Latency SLAs are impossible to reason about.

**Pinned**: p1-p10 mean = 3,614 ns, overall mean = 5,600 ns → **1.5× spread**. The whole distribution is tight. Your worst typical case (p90-p99 = 7,753 ns) is only 2× your best case. Predictable, plannable.

For bursty workloads that's the real argument for pinning — not "is it faster" but **"is it predictable."** A burst arrives, you need to drain N messages. Unpinned: some drain at 400 ns, some at 7 µs — your tail latency is dominated by which mode the scheduler happened to be in when the burst hit. Pinned: every message costs ~5.6 µs ± 1.3 µs, you can budget for it.

For your actor system this means: if you're targeting deterministic latency (which a state-machine-based actor system with explicit back-pressure implies you are), **pin your threads**. Accept the slightly higher floor (3.5 µs vs 200 ns best-case) in exchange for knowing exactly what you'll get. The unpinned fast path is a gift from the scheduler you can't rely on.

> DEVELOPER

Last Q for today, we now see the symmetry why was that so hard to come to, was my expectation out of wack and if rather than show the "range" how would you label the rows with a single value that you think I'd like, I don't see any I like.

> AGENT

**Why was it hard?** Your expectation was fine — you wanted symmetric bands. The difficulty was that **percentiles are points, bands are ranges**, and we were trying to shoehorn a range into a point label. "p99" is a single boundary value at the 99th percentile. When you associate a *band* with it, you have to choose: does p99 own the band *below* it (90→99% = 9%) or *above* it (99→100% = 1%)? Neither choice gives both tails as 1% simultaneously. The symmetric display only worked once we stopped pretending rows are points and made them explicitly ranges (min-p1, p99-max).

**Single-value labels?** Honestly, I don't think a good one exists — which is probably why you don't see one you like either. The candidates:

| approach | example labels | problem |
|---|---|---|
| upper boundary | p1, p10, ..., p99, max | back to the asymmetry confusion |
| lower boundary | min, p1, ..., p90, p99 | same problem, other end |
| midpoint percentile | p0.5, p5, p15, ..., p95, p99.5 | awkward, unfamiliar |
| band fraction | 1%, 9%, 10%, ..., 9%, 1% | duplicates (many 10%s) |
| descriptive | bottom-1%, low-9%, ..., top-1% | verbose, not standard |

The range label (min-p1, p1-p10, ..., p99-max) is the only one that's both unambiguous and symmetric. It's a little wider but it's honest — a band IS two boundaries, and pretending otherwise is what caused all the confusion.

Each band is\ndefined by two consecutive percentile boundaries (e.g. min→p1,\np1→p10, …, p99→max) and displays first, last, count, mean, and\nadjusted mean — computed by iterating the histogram's recorded\nbuckets and accumulating per-band stats.\n\n### Why\n\nPercentile rows associate a single boundary value with a label\n(e.g. \"p99 = 10,095 ns\"). When we tried to add per-band sample\ncounts, neither direction of association (prev→current or\ncurrent→next) produced symmetric tail counts: p1 and p99 always\nended up with different band widths depending on which direction\nwas chosen. This caused persistent confusion about what \"p99's\ncount\" meant.\n\nMaking the *band* the row (not the *point*) resolved the\nasymmetry: min-p1 and p99-max are both 1% of total by\nconstruction. The range labels are wider but unambiguous — a\nband IS two boundaries and pretending otherwise was the source\nof all the confusion.\n\n### Change\n\n- `print_histogram` now iterates `hist.iter_recorded()` to\n  accumulate per-band first/last/count/sum. Each histogram\n  bucket is assigned to the band containing its midpoint rank.\n- Band boundaries: 0%, 1%, 10%, 20%, …, 90%, 99%, 100%.\n  Labels: min-p1, p1-p10, p10-p20, …, p90-p99, p99-max.\n- Display columns: first, last, count, mean, adjusted.\n  Auto-sized column widths (same pattern as 0.3.3).\n- Whole-histogram mean and stdev shown below the band table\n  without a count column.\n- Removed the old `Row` struct and its constructors — rendering\n  is now done via a local `BandRow` struct inside\n  `print_histogram`.\n\nSingle-step bump 0.3.4 → 0.3.5."
}
